The debate matters practically: if schemas are symbolic, they can in principle be deliberately constructed through explicit instruction; if they are subsymbolic emergent patterns, explicit instruction may not be the most efficient building tool.

> [!tension] **Tension 1: Cognitive Load vs. Desirable Difficulty** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Position A (Sweller/CLT):** Working memory is severely limited; learning materials should be designed to minimize extraneous cognitive load to free capacity for germane processing. Effortful, confusing presentations (including some interleaving and spacing effects) may overload working memory, especially for novices.
> 
> **Position B (Bjork/DD):** Conditions that feel difficult and produce worse performance during learning often produce superior long-term retention and transfer. Reducing cognitive load to support smooth within-session performance may undermine the very processes that build durable knowledge.
> 
> **Current state:** The tension is partially resolved by expertise: CLT's load-reduction recommendations apply primarily to novices for whom intrinsic load is high; desirable difficulties produce their benefits primarily when learners have sufficient prior knowledge to engage constructively with the difficulty. The resolution is conditional on learner expertise — advice appropriate for novices differs from advice appropriate for advanced learners.
> 
> **This report's stance:** Both frameworks are right in their target populations. For PKB-based strategic reading (targeting intermediate to advanced learners, self-curated reading material), desirable difficulties are primary; CLT scaffolding remains relevant for genuinely unfamiliar material.

> [!tension] **Tension 2: Retrieval Practice vs. Constructive Elaboration** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Position A:** Retrieval practice is the most powerful learning technique, outperforming concept mapping, elaboration, and other constructive strategies (Karpicke and Blunt, 2011). Educators should prioritize testing over study strategies.
> 
> **Position B:** Deep understanding requires constructive elaboration that retrieval practice alone does not provide. Retrieval practice strengthens whatever is encoded, but if encoding is shallow (surface features rather than deep structure), retrieval practice just reinforces shallow knowledge.
> 
> **Current state:** The evidence shows retrieval practice wins on retention metrics; elaboration wins on some transfer and deep-structure measures. The most effective combination is elaborative encoding followed by retrieval practice — encoding depth and retrieval consolidation are complementary rather than competing.
> 
> **This report's stance:** Both are necessary components of the complete system; the sequencing matters (elaboration before retrieval produces better outcomes than retrieval before elaboration for new material).


















## Spaced Repetition Seeds

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is elaborative interrogation, and what is the specific question format it uses?
> 
> **A:** Elaborative interrogation is a study strategy in which the learner generates answers to "why" questions about factual material being studied — specifically, "Why is it true that [fact]?" The defining feature is the causal-explanatory demand, which distinguishes EI from other question-asking strategies that use "what" or "when" formats.
> 
> **Source:** Phase I / Lexicon A
> 
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> 
> **Type:** Definition
> 
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #reading-strategies, #learning-science

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between a textbase and a situation model? Give a concrete reading example.
> 
> **A:** A textbase is the propositional representation of what the text asserts, stripped of exact wording — it supports verbatim recall and close-paraphrase recognition. A situation model is the integrated representation of the subject matter itself, constructed by fusing textbase propositions with prior knowledge schemas — it supports inference, transfer, and novel application. Example: a reader who can recall that "photosynthesis converts light into glucose" has a textbase; one who can explain why plants use glucose as an energy carrier rather than storing ATP directly has a situation model.
> 
> **Source:** Phase II
> 
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> 
> **Type:** Distinction
> 
> **Tags:** #situation-model, #textbase, #text-comprehension, #van-dijk-kintsch

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Comprehension Gateway Mechanism, and what are its three structural components?
> 
> **A:** The Comprehension Gateway Mechanism is the process by which EI produces comprehension benefits: a Register 2 "why" question makes textbase-level retrieval insufficient, forcing situation model construction. Its three components are: (1) Demand-Exceeds-Textbase Structure — the question cannot be answered from the text; (2) Integration Anchor Requirement — construction requires connecting text propositions to prior knowledge schemas; (3) Construction Premium — generating the connection produces richer encoding than receiving it.
> 
> **Source:** Phase VI, Synthesis
> 
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> 
> **Type:** Process
> 
> **Tags:** #comprehension-gateway, #elaborative-interrogation, #situation-model, #original-construct

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** Why does prior knowledge moderate elaborative interrogation effectiveness, and is this moderation peripheral or structural?
> 
> **A:** Prior knowledge moderation is structural, not peripheral. Situation model construction — the process EI invokes — requires prior knowledge schemas as integration anchors: points of connection to which new text propositions can be attached within a causal-relational framework. A learner without relevant prior knowledge cannot execute the gateway mechanism because they lack the integration anchors that construction requires. This is why EI effects collapse (not merely diminish) when prior knowledge is absent, and why providing background reading before EI instruction partially restores EI benefits.
> 
> **Source:** Phases III-IV
> 
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> 
> **Type:** Connection
> 
> **Tags:** #prior-knowledge, #elaborative-interrogation, #integration-anchor, #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Dual Register Diagnostic, and how is a Register 1 question distinguished from a Register 2 question?
> 
> **A:** The Dual Register Diagnostic classifies EI questions by their answer level: Register 1 questions can be answered by someone who knows only the text (textbase-satisfiable); Register 2 questions require world knowledge not in the text (situation model-requiring). The diagnostic test is: "Could a reader with only this text, and no other knowledge, answer this question?" If yes: Register 1. If no: Register 2. Register 1 questions do not function as comprehension gateways and produce weak EI effects.
> 
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.2
> 
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> 
> **Type:** Distinction
> 
> **Tags:** #dual-register, #elaborative-interrogation, #question-design, #original-construct

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation advantage in EI, and why does it specifically arise in elaborative interrogation rather than from the generic generation effect alone?
> 
> **A:** The generation advantage is the finding that self-produced elaborations outperform experimenter-provided elaborations in EI. While the generic generation effect (Slamecka & Graf, 1978) contributes, EI's generation advantage also includes a specific construction premium: generating a causal connection requires actively traversing the conceptual path from text proposition to prior knowledge schema, reinforcing both the path and the endpoint. Provided elaborations allow recognition of a connection; generated elaborations require its construction — and the construction is the learning.
> 
> **Source:** Phases III and IV
> 
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> 
> **Type:** Process
> 
> **Tags:** #generation-effect, #elaborative-interrogation, #construction-premium

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** How should elaborative interrogation be integrated into PKB note-building practice, and what is the key design criterion for EI-style note prompts?
> 
> **A:** In PKB practice, EI should be applied by: (1) formulating Register 2 "why" questions as you read — ones whose answers require constructing causal connections, not locating sentences; (2) generating answers before consulting other sources; (3) writing notes at the situation model level ("X causes Y because [mechanism]") rather than the textbase level ("X causes Y"); (4) tagging unanswerable questions as prior knowledge gaps requiring background reading. The key design criterion for note prompts is the Dual Register Diagnostic: a prompt should be answerable only by constructing a causal-functional account, not by paraphrasing the source.
> 
> **Source:** Phase V, Best Practice callout
> 
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> 
> **Type:** Application
> 
> **Tags:** #pkb-practice, #elaborative-interrogation, #note-design, #reading-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Demand-Architecture Isomorphism, and what does it predict about the relationship between task design and learning depth?
> 
> **A:** The Demand-Architecture Isomorphism is the principle that the level of cognitive architecture engaged in processing is isomorphic to the level required to satisfy the task's demand structure. It predicts that: the level of understanding produced by a learning activity is determined by the minimum representational level needed to answer the task, not by the learner's subjective effort. A textbase-satisfiable question produces textbase encoding regardless of effort; a situation-model-requiring question forces situation model encoding. This implies that the most important instructional design decision is not "how difficult" but "what level of architecture does answering this demand?"
> 
> **Source:** Phase VI
> 
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> 
> **Type:** Definition
> 
> **Tags:** #demand-architecture, #instructional-design, #situation-model, #original-construct

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does elaborative interrogation connect to the desirable difficulties framework, and what specifically is "desirable" about EI's difficulty?
> 
> **A:** EI is a desirable difficulty (Bjork, 1994) because the cognitive demand it imposes is precisely the demand of situation model construction — the load that produces durable, transferable understanding. But EI's difficulty is not generically desirable; it is specifically targeted: the question format structurally blocks the textbase shortcut and requires exactly the kind of cognitive work (integration of text propositions with prior schemas) that produces rich encoding. The difficulty is desirable because it *is* the mechanism of the benefit — not an unfortunate cost of a useful technique but the productive struggle of construction itself.
> 
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.4
> 
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> 
> **Type:** Connection
> 
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties, #elaborative-interrogation, #productive-struggle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-reading-focused-analysis-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise reversal effect for EI, and why does the Comprehension Gateway Mechanism predict it?
> 
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect for EI is the finding that EI benefit diminishes as learners become expert in a domain — eventually becoming redundant or counterproductive. The Comprehension Gateway Mechanism predicts this: experts already construct situation models automatically during reading because their prior knowledge schemas are rich and activated rapidly. The gateway mechanism is no longer needed — the door is already open. Requiring experts to generate elaborations for material they already have situation models for produces redundancy effects (wasted processing resources on unnecessary elaboration), not learning benefits.
> 
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.2
> 
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> 
> **Type:** Process
> 
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal, #elaborative-interrogation, #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the difference between a textbase and a situation model in reading comprehension?
> **A:** **A:** A *textbase* is a propositional semantic representation of what the text explicitly says — a network of propositions derived from the text's surface content. A *situation model* is a richer mental model of the actual situation, event, or concept described by the text, incorporating prior knowledge, inferences, spatial relationships, and causal structure. Situation models support transfer and problem-solving; textbase representations support paraphrase and recognition.
> **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #reading-comprehension #cognitive-science

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Fluency Illusion, and why does it undermine metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** **A:** The Fluency Illusion is the systematic tendency to interpret *ease of text processing* (perceptual fluency) as evidence of *comprehension depth*, when fluency and comprehension are not reliably related. Re-reading increases fluency without necessarily improving situation model quality — but the increased fluency feels like improved understanding. This creates false metacognitive confidence and is a primary driver of the passive re-reading trap.
> **Source:** Section 8.2 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What are the three sub-types of metacognitive knowledge, and which is most predictive of strategic reading proficiency?
> **A:** **A:** (1) *Declarative* — knowing *what* strategies exist; (2) *Procedural* — knowing *how* to execute strategies; (3) *Conditional* — knowing *when* and *why* to use which strategy. Conditional metacognitive knowledge is most predictive of advanced reading proficiency and hardest to develop through instruction.
> **Source:** Section 3.1 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #metacognition #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Matthew Effect in reading, and what does this report claim about its interaction with metacognitive monitoring?
> **A:** **A:** The Matthew Effect describes how early reading advantages (vocabulary, comprehension) compound over time — rich get richer, poor get poorer in literacy development. This report's synthesis argues that the Matthew Effect creates a *double failure trap*: poor domain knowledge simultaneously degrades comprehension (less effective schema application) and degrades metacognitive monitoring accuracy (schemas needed for monitoring failures are absent). Both deficits compound each other.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #matthew-effect #metacognition #prior-knowledge

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** According to Dunlosky et al. (2013), which learning strategies have high utility? Which have low utility?
> **A:** **A:** **High utility:** retrieval practice (practice testing) and spaced repetition. **Moderate utility:** elaborative interrogation, self-explanation, interleaving. **Low utility:** re-reading and highlighting. The high-utility strategies are underused by most students despite being most effective; the low-utility strategies are overused because they feel effective (due to Fluency Illusion).
> **Source:** Section 5.2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #learning-strategies #retrieval-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is Reciprocal Teaching, and what four strategies does it involve?
> **A:** **A:** Reciprocal Teaching (Palincsar & Brown, 1984) is a scaffolded reading comprehension intervention involving small groups rotating through four metacognitive strategies: (1) **Summarizing** — capturing main points to check integration; (2) **Question generating** — formulating key questions to promote elaboration; (3) **Clarifying** — addressing confusing terms or passages; (4) **Predicting** — using text structure to anticipate upcoming content. Each strategy constitutes a component of the metacognitive monitoring-and-control cycle.
> **Source:** Section 7.2 | **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #reciprocal-teaching #reading-instruction #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Zone of Proximal Development, and how does it apply to reading comprehension instruction?
> **A:** **A:** Vygotsky's ZPD defines the range of tasks a learner cannot manage independently but can manage with appropriate support. In reading instruction, the ZPD identifies texts and tasks where comprehension is possible with scaffolding but would fail without it. Optimal instruction targets the ZPD — neither too easy (no challenge) nor too difficult (incomprehensible even with support). Scaffolding is gradually reduced as the learner's independent competence develops (I-We-You progression).
> **Difficulty:** Basic | **Tags:** #vygotsky #scaffolding #zone-of-proximal-development

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is conditional metacognitive knowledge, and why is it more powerful than declarative or procedural metacognitive knowledge for reading?
> **A:** **A:** Conditional metacognitive knowledge is knowing *when* and *why* to apply specific reading strategies — understanding the match between strategy type, text type, and comprehension goal. It is more powerful than declarative knowledge (knowing strategies exist) or procedural knowledge (knowing how to execute them) because it enables strategic flexibility: deploying the right strategy in the right context rather than applying favorite strategies indiscriminately. This strategy-selection capacity is the metacognitive master skill.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge #conditional-knowledge #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is elaborative interrogation, and what mechanism explains its effectiveness?
> **A:** **A:** Elaborative interrogation is a reading strategy involving self-generated "Why is this true?" questions after encountering factual or explanatory claims. Its effectiveness is explained by multiple converging mechanisms: (1) prior knowledge activation (retrieving related schemas); (2) semantic elaboration (connecting the claim to a knowledge network, creating multiple retrieval pathways); (3) inference generation (producing causal inferences beyond text information); (4) comprehension monitoring (inability to generate a plausible "why" signals comprehension failure).
> **Source:** Section 5.2 | **Difficulty:** Intermediate | **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation #deep-processing

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[developing-reading-comprehension-and-metacognition-for-academic-texts-foundational-report-2026-04-08]])*
> **Q:** Q:** What is the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control model, and what is the key distinction it draws?
> **A:** **A:** The Nelson-Narens (1990) model describes metacognitive functioning as a two-level system: the *object level* (the actual cognitive reading processes — word recognition, sentence parsing, situation model construction) and the *meta level* (the monitoring and control system operating on object-level processes). *Monitoring* = information flow from object to meta level (the meta system's observation of comprehension state). *Control* = information flow from meta to object level (executive regulation of reading behavior in response to monitoring). Key insight: monitoring and control are distinct — readers can monitor failures without deploying appropriate control responses.
> **Source:** Section 3.2 | **Difficulty:** Advanced | **Tags:** #metacognition #nelson-narens #monitoring-control

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is elaborative interrogation, and what distinguishes it from simply re-reading or questioning a text?
> **A:** EI is a generative learning strategy in which the learner answers "why is this true?" questions about specific factual propositions, drawing on prior knowledge to produce causal-explanatory accounts. It is distinguished by its generative character (answers must be produced, not read), its why-format constraint (causal-explanatory, not just reflective), and its prior knowledge activation requirement.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #learning-strategy, #definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** How does elaborative interrogation differ from self-explanation in content target and mechanism?
> **A:** EI targets factual-relational propositions ("X has property Y") and produces causal accounts of why facts hold; self-explanation targets procedural or causal content and produces accounts of how processes work. The mechanisms partially overlap (both involve generative processing) but EI more directly depends on prior semantic knowledge for answer generation.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #self-explanation, #distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Mechanism
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three components of the mechanism chain linking EI execution to superior retention?
> **A:** (1) Cued prior knowledge retrieval: the why-question activates relevant prior knowledge networks; (2) elaborative integration: the retrieved prior knowledge is connected to the target proposition, forming a causal account; (3) networked encoding: the target proposition is stored within the activated knowledge network, providing multiple retrieval pathways.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #mechanism, #encoding

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Theory Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does levels-of-processing theory explain EI's effectiveness? What specifically does EI guarantee that passive re-reading does not?
> **A:** LOP theory holds that encoding durability is a function of semantic depth and elaborative richness. EI structurally guarantees semantic-level processing: generating a why-answer necessarily engages with relational, causal, meaningful features of the proposition. Passive re-reading can remain at the surface-feature level, producing the fluency illusion without deep encoding.
> **Source:** Phase III
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #levels-of-processing, #elaborative-interrogation, #deep-processing

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Prior Knowledge Moderator
> **A:** **Q:** Why does elaborative interrogation fail (or backfire) when prior knowledge is insufficient? What is the specific risk?
> **A:** When prior knowledge is insufficient, learners cannot generate accurate elaborations. If they generate inaccurate causal accounts with confidence, these accounts may be encoded alongside or instead of the target fact, creating misconceptions. The accuracy constraint means EI's effectiveness depends on the quality, not merely the quantity, of generated elaborations.
> **Source:** Phases III, IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #prior-knowledge, #accuracy-constraint, #elaborative-interrogation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are Dunlosky et al.'s (2013) utility ratings for EI relative to retrieval practice and re-reading?
> **A:** EI received "moderate utility" — above re-reading and highlighting (low utility), but below retrieval practice and distributed practice (high utility). The moderate rating reflects genuine but conditioned efficacy: EI is reliably effective when prior knowledge supports accurate elaboration generation, but not universally effective.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #dunlosky, #learning-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Mechanism
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinctiveness hypothesis in EI research, and how does it complement the integration mechanism?
> **A:** The distinctiveness hypothesis (McDaniel & Donnelly, 1996) holds that EI generates distinctive memory traces — encoded with a unique constellation of semantic features — that are resistant to interference. Distinctiveness accounts for why EI items resist intrusion from similar information; integration (connecting to prior knowledge networks) accounts for why they have multiple retrieval pathways. Together they produce both resistance to interference and enhanced cue-based retrieval.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #distinctiveness, #elaborative-interrogation, #mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "accuracy-activation dialectic" and why does it matter for expert use of EI?
> **A:** The accuracy-activation dialectic (original synthesis) names the tension between two facts: (1) EI is most powerful when prior knowledge is richest; but (2) rich prior knowledge creates the risk that fluent but inaccurate schemata will distort encoding of genuinely discrepant new information. Expert EI use requires maintaining calibrated skepticism toward self-generated elaborations — neither assuming accuracy because elaboration felt fluent nor abandoning elaboration because the domain is difficult.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #accuracy-constraint, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** How does EI contribute specifically to situation model construction during reading?
> **A:** Situation models have multiple dimensions (spatial, temporal, causal, referential). EI contributes specifically to the causal dimension: by generating why-answers, the reader explicitly constructs the causal network linking text propositions. This produces a more causally coherent mental model of the described state of affairs, enabling inference and transfer beyond verbatim text recall.
> **Source:** Phase IV
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #situation-model, #elaborative-interrogation, #comprehension

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** Why is EI described as complementary rather than competitive with retrieval practice? At what points in the learning cycle does each strategy operate?
> **A:** EI primarily operates during *initial encoding* — it deepens and enriches the quality of the initial memory trace by integrating new propositions into the prior knowledge network. Retrieval practice primarily operates during *consolidation* — strengthening already-encoded information through active reconstruction against resistance. Combining EI (for deep initial encoding) with subsequent spaced retrieval practice (for consolidation) should outperform either strategy alone.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice, #elaborative-interrogation, #learning-sequence

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why is elaborative interrogation structurally resistant to it?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the metacognitive error of mistaking the subjective ease of re-processing familiar-seeming material for genuine comprehension or retention. Re-reading produces fluency without encoding depth, generating a false sense of understanding. EI is resistant to the fluency illusion because the generative demand — producing a causal answer — exposes the gap between fluency and genuine comprehension: a learner cannot feel fluent about a why-answer they cannot actually produce.
> **Source:** Phase III, V
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion, #elaborative-interrogation, #metacognitive-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** In the PKB context, what distinguishes a note produced through EI (a "synthesis artifact") from a transcription note? Why does this matter?
> **A:** A transcription note records what the source said; a synthesis artifact (EI note) records *why the source's claim is true*, connecting the new proposition to existing PKB nodes through generated causal accounts. The difference is epistemic: synthesis artifacts are integrated into the knowledge graph (with wiki-links to activated prior knowledge nodes), while transcription artifacts are isolated. EI notes add both a memory advantage (networked encoding) and a knowledge graph advantage (new edges between existing nodes).
> **Source:** Phase V, Appendix 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #pkb, #elaborative-interrogation, #synthesis

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What does the ICAP framework predict about EI's effectiveness, and why?
> **A:** ICAP predicts that constructive activities (which EI exemplifies) produce superior learning to active, passive, or interactive activities, because constructive activities require the learner to generate knowledge outputs that go beyond the presented information. EI specifically involves generating a causal explanation not contained in the text, placing it squarely in the constructive category. ICAP's constructive engagement hypothesis directly predicts EI's moderate-to-high utility rating.
> **Source:** Phase II
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #ICAP-framework, #elaborative-interrogation, #constructive-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[elaborative-interrogation-foundational-report-2026-03-30]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What does the Matthew Effect imply for the pedagogical deployment of elaborative interrogation?
> **A:** The Matthew Effect describes cumulative advantage: those who have more knowledge gain more from EI (richer prior knowledge → better elaborations → deeper encoding), while those who have less gain less and risk misconception formation. This means EI functions as a *knowledge amplifier* rather than a knowledge equalizer: deploying it without prerequisite knowledge-building may widen the gap between learners with strong and weak domain backgrounds. Equitable deployment requires knowledge-building scaffolding before EI introduction in any domain.
> **Source:** Phase V
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #matthew-effect, #prior-knowledge, #elaborative-interrogation, #equity

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 1 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is comprehension monitoring, and at what levels does it operate?
> **A:** The continuous metacognitive evaluation of text representation adequacy against multiple standards (lexical, propositional, coherence, situation-model). Failures at any level constitute candidate triggers for regulatory response. Most poor comprehenders monitor at lexical/surface levels but neglect coherence and situation-model levels.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.1; Phase IV, Section 4.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #reading, #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 2 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between the textbase and the situation model, and why does the distinction matter for reading comprehension?
> **A:** The textbase is a propositional representation of what the text explicitly states; the situation model is the reader's integrated mental representation of the state of affairs described, enriched by inference and prior knowledge. Only situation-model construction constitutes deep comprehension; textbase representation supports only surface paraphrasing.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #reading, #situation-model, #textbase

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 3 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and what cognitive mechanism produces it?
> **A:** The misattribution of surface-reading fluency (decoding ease, lexical retrieval speed) to comprehension depth. Produced by the monitoring system's reliance on processing ease as a comprehension signal — but fluency carries information about surface-level processing, not situation-model construction. Cause: monitoring operates at the wrong level of text representation.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #fluency-illusion, #monitoring-failure

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 4 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the Nelson-Narens monitoring-control architecture as applied to reading.
> **A:** Object level = direct text processing (decoding, parsing, semantic integration). Meta level = metacognitive monitoring of object-level processes. Monitoring flows upward: represents state of comprehension. Control flows downward: adjusts object-level strategy in response. Effective comprehension monitoring requires: (1) accurate monitoring signals, (2) reliable monitoring-control coupling, (3) appropriate control responses.
> **Source:** Phase II, Section 2.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #nelson-narens, #metacognitive-monitoring, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 5 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between metacognitive monitoring failure and motivational disengagement in the context of reading comprehension deficits?
> **A:** Monitoring failure = the reader genuinely does not detect comprehension breakdown (cognitive deficit in the detection mechanism). Motivational disengagement = the reader detects failure but suppresses regulatory response (motivationally driven suppression, often to protect self-efficacy). These two pathways require different instructional responses.
> **Source:** Phase VII, Section 3 (Tensions)
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #motivation, #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 6 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What are declarative, procedural, and conditional metacognitive knowledge, and how do they apply to reading strategy instruction?
> **A:** Declarative = knowing *that* (strategy names, self-knowledge); Procedural = knowing *how* (strategy execution); Conditional = knowing *when and why* (appropriateness conditions). Reading strategy instruction most commonly develops only declarative and procedural knowledge; conditional knowledge — the most critical for transfer — is most commonly neglected.
> **Source:** Phase III, Section 3.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognitive-knowledge, #strategy-instruction, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 7 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four strategies in reciprocal teaching and what metacognitive function does each perform?
> **A:** (1) Predicting — activates prior knowledge, establishes comprehension goals; (2) Questioning — enforces self-monitoring via explicit comprehension question generation; (3) Clarifying — comprehension monitoring and fix-up strategy deployment for detected failures; (4) Summarizing — coherence evaluation at the discourse level, tests situation model construction.
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #reciprocal-teaching, #metacognitive-strategies, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 8 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does Verbose Efficiency Theory connect decoding fluency to reading comprehension via the working memory mechanism?
> **A:** Inefficient decoding (non-automatic lexical access) consumes working memory resources with lexical-phonological retrieval, leaving insufficient capacity for comprehension processes (coherence monitoring, inference generation, situation-model construction). Therefore, reading fluency is a necessary (though not sufficient) condition for metacognitive comprehension monitoring in demanding texts.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #verbal-efficiency-theory, #working-memory, #reading-fluency

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 9 — Distinction
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes effective annotation strategies from passive highlighting in terms of metacognitive engagement?
> **A:** Passive highlighting = topographic marking of verbatim text; associated with fluency illusion rather than comprehension monitoring. Effective annotation = content-transforming marks (questions, summaries, connections, evaluations) that require comprehension evaluation, inference generation, and prior-knowledge integration. Effective annotation externalizes the metacognitive regulatory dialogue; passive highlighting does not.
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.4
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #annotation, #metacognition, #reading-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 10 — Process
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four stages of effective fix-up strategy deployment during reading?
> **A:** (1) Signal detection — monitoring detects comprehension failure; (2) Failure diagnosis — reader identifies the level/type of failure (lexical, propositional, coherence, situation-model); (3) Strategy selection — conditional metacognitive knowledge guides response selection; (4) Strategy execution and evaluation — strategy deployed and monitoring re-evaluates whether failure is resolved.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fix-up-strategy, #comprehension-monitoring, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 11 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the Matthew Effect in reading relate to metacognitive monitoring capacity?
> **A:** Poor readers have reduced metacognitive monitoring capacity because demanding decoding exhausts working memory; reduced monitoring leads to reduced comprehension; reduced comprehension means less schema building and vocabulary growth, which keeps decoding demanding and keeps monitoring capacity suppressed. The failure cycle is: poor decoding → high cognitive load → suppressed monitoring → comprehension failure → reduced vocabulary and schema growth → continued poor decoding.
> **Source:** Phase IV, Section 4.5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #matthew-effect, #metacognition, #reading, #cognitive-load

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 12 — Definition
> **A:** **Q:** What does "self-regulated reading" mean and how does it relate to Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model?
> **A:** Self-regulated reading is metacognitive, goal-directed, and strategically enacted text comprehension. Maps onto Zimmerman's three phases: (1) Forethought = goal-setting, prior knowledge activation, strategy planning; (2) Performance = comprehension monitoring, strategy deployment, attention regulation; (3) Self-reflection = outcome evaluation, attribution, strategy adaptation. Distinguishes reading as self-directed enterprise from externally regulated strategy use.
> **Source:** Phase III, Section 3.4; Phase II, Section 2.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning, #reading, #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 13 — Application
> **A:** **Q:** What is the KWL strategy, and which metacognitive reading functions does each component serve?
> **A:** K (What do I *Know*?) = prior knowledge activation and establishing comprehension baseline; W (What do I *Want* to know?) = goal-setting and directing monitoring attention; L (What have I *Learned*?) = comprehension evaluation, gap detection, and situation model assessment. Collectively: the KWL sequence operationalizes the forethought-monitoring-self-reflection SRL cycle for a single reading session.
> **Source:** Phase V, Section 5.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #KWL-strategy, #metacognition, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[metacognition-and-reading-foundational-report-2026-03-31]])*
> **Q:** Seed 14 — Connection
> **A:** **Q:** How does the elaborative interrogation strategy operationalize the depth-of-processing principle in reading?
> **A:** Depth-of-processing principle: semantic/elaborative processing produces more durable encoding than surface processing. Elaborative interrogation operationalizes deep processing by requiring the reader to generate *why/how* explanations for text propositions, thereby enforcing semantic elaboration and prior-knowledge integration. The strategy converts passive proposition reception into active inferential construction — the functional equivalent of forced deep processing.
> **Source:** Phase III, Section 3.5; Phase VII, Lexicon
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #elaborative-interrogation, #levels-of-processing, #reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the distinction between textbase and situation model in Kintsch's reading comprehension model?
> **A:** The textbase is a coherent propositional representation of *what the text says* — the literal semantic content of the text's sentences. The situation model is a mental representation of *what the text describes* — the state of affairs in the world or domain the text refers to. Genuine comprehension requires situation model construction; textbase representation alone supports recognition but not transfer or integration.
> **Source:** Section 2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #comprehension #reading-theory #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion and why is it motivationally significant?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the metacognitive error of mistaking the ease of text processing (reading fluency) for depth of comprehension (situation model construction). It is motivationally significant because it prevents deployment of deeper processing strategies (if the reader believes they understand, they stop working to understand), produces false assessment confidence, and when assessment performance fails, this failure is misattributed to ability rather than monitoring — reinforcing entity theories and performance-avoidance goals.
> **Source:** Sections 2 & 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #metacognition #fluency-illusion #comprehension-monitoring

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three basic psychological needs in SDT, and how does each apply specifically to academic reading?
> **A:** (1) **Autonomy** — the need to experience reading as self-initiated and volitionally chosen. In reading: supporting autonomy means providing genuine rationales, meaningful choice within required content, and framing reading for personal understanding rather than external compliance. (2) **Competence** — the need to experience efficacy and mastery. In reading: satisfied by successful comprehension experiences, progressive difficulty calibration, and strategy training that produces genuine understanding. (3) **Relatedness** — the need for connection with others. In reading: satisfied by shared reading practices, discussion communities, and meaningful connection of reading content to valued relationships and communities.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #SDT #basic-psychological-needs #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish mastery-approach, mastery-avoidance, performance-approach, and performance-avoidance goals in the context of academic reading.
> **A:** **Mastery-approach:** Reading to develop genuine understanding; predicts deep processing, persistence, positive affect. **Mastery-avoidance:** Reading to avoid failure-to-understand, driven by comprehension anxiety; predicts self-monitoring and persistence from fear. **Performance-approach:** Reading to demonstrate superior comprehension relative to others; predicts adequate assessed performance but surface strategic reading. **Performance-avoidance:** Reading to avoid appearing incompetent; predicts avoidance, surface processing, learned helplessness — most consistently negative outcome profile.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #achievement-goal-theory #goal-orientation #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the logic of the three-tier strategy hierarchy and what does it mean to "start at the wrong tier"?
> **A:** The hierarchy sequences strategies according to prerequisite logic: Tier 1 (cognitive foundation) creates conditions Tier 2 presupposes; Tier 2 (comprehension strategies) creates conditions Tier 3 presupposes. Starting at the wrong tier means applying strategies that require conditions not yet in place. Example: teaching elaborative interrogation (Tier 2) to a reader with profound vocabulary gaps (Tier 1 deficit) will fail because the reader cannot generate meaningful elaborations when basic comprehension is blocked by unknown vocabulary. The diagnostic must precede the prescription.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #strategy-hierarchy #reading-motivation #intervention-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is self-explanation as a reading strategy and why is it more effective than summarization?
> **A:** Self-explanation involves generating explanatory verbalizations or written articulations of what a passage means in the reader's own words, how it relates to prior content and knowledge, and what gaps remain. It is more effective than summarization because it requires construction of causal relationships and inferential connections that the text may not state explicitly — forcing situation model construction rather than accurate textbase restatement. Summarization can be achieved at the textbase level; genuine self-explanation requires situation model access.
> **Source:** Section 6.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #self-explanation #comprehension-strategy #situation-model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is Zimmerman's cyclical SRL model and what are the three phases applied to academic reading?
> **A:** **Forethought phase:** Before reading — goal setting (what do I want to genuinely understand?), strategic planning (which strategies are appropriate for this text?), motivational self-management (intrinsic goal activation, prior knowledge preparation). **Performance phase:** During reading — strategy deployment, comprehension monitoring, fix-up strategy selection and execution when failures are detected. **Self-reflection phase:** After reading — self-evaluation of comprehension quality, causal attribution of successes and failures, adaptive planning for the next reading session. The cycle makes self-regulated reading self-improving rather than repetitively consistent.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #self-regulated-learning #metacognition #reading-self-regulation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is interest theory's four-phase model and what distinguishes situational from individual interest?
> **A:** Four phases: (1) Triggered situational interest — externally triggered by environmental features; (2) Maintained situational interest — sustained by engaging content value; (3) Emerging individual interest — beginning of stored value and re-engagement tendency; (4) Well-developed individual interest — stable, self-sustaining predisposition to engage. **Situational interest** is triggered by specific contextual features (an engaging text, a surprising demonstration) and dissipates when those features are absent. **Individual interest** is a relatively stable personal predisposition that motivates seeking out domain-relevant content across contexts, independent of specific environmental triggers.
> **Source:** Section 3.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #interest-theory #situational-interest #individual-interest #reading-motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Why is retrieval practice more effective than re-reading for long-term retention, and how should it be applied during academic reading?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is more effective because the act of retrieving strengthens the memory trace through effortful reconstruction (a desirable difficulty that enhances encoding), whereas re-reading produces recognition familiarity (high processing fluency) without effortful retrieval — it improves performance on immediate tests but not long-term retention. **Application during reading:** After completing a section, close or cover the text and write from memory the main points and key arguments. Check accuracy against the text. The gaps identified during retrieval attempts are precisely the content requiring focused re-reading or further elaboration.
> **Source:** Sections 6.3 and 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice #spaced-repetition #desirable-difficulties #reading-strategy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivated-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Motivational-Cognitive Reading Stack and what diagnostic implication does it carry?
> **A:** The Stack integrates four systems that must each meet threshold development for motivated academic reading to be self-sustaining: **Layer 1** (Cognitive Pre-Conditions: fluency, vocabulary, working memory management, prior knowledge), **Layer 2** (Comprehension-Generating Strategies: self-explanation, elaborative interrogation, retrieval practice), **Layer 3** (Motivation Architecture: autonomous regulation, mastery goals, intrinsic goal framing, individual interest). **Diagnostic implication:** Identify the lowest layer where the system is failing. A reader who cannot sustain motivation despite good comprehension skills needs Layer 3 work. A reader who is motivated but comprehends shallowly needs Layer 2 strategy training. A reader whose strategies fail needs Layer 1 development. Starting intervention at the correct layer is essential.
> **Source:** Section 6.4 original synthesis
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #motivational-cognitive-stack #original-synthesis #reading-motivation #diagnostic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Motivational Deployment Problem, and how does it differ from strategy ignorance or strategic incapacity?
> 
> **Answer:** The Motivational Deployment Problem is the structural gap between knowing effective learning strategies work and consistently deploying them. It differs from strategy ignorance (not knowing what works) and strategic incapacity (lacking the skill) in that the learner has both knowledge and capacity but fails to deploy due to motivational barriers — primarily the aversive phenomenological experience of the strategies that work best.
> 
> **Source:** Section 1: The Motivation-Strategy Nexus
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #motivation #learning-science #strategy-deployment

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** How does autonomous motivation differ from controlled motivation in its effect on the SRL monitoring phase?
> 
> **Answer:** Autonomous motivation supports honest monitoring: comprehension failure is experienced as information, not threat, enabling accurate self-assessment. Controlled motivation produces ego-defensive monitoring: learners subjectively upgrade comprehension estimates under introjected regulation (where self-esteem depends on performance), filtering out monitoring signals that would damage self-concept. Same monitoring capacity; opposite willingness to attend to what it reveals.
> 
> **Source:** Section 5.2: Where SDT Intersects the SRL Cycle
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #self-regulated-learning #metacognition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Process):** Walk through the hierarchical order of the seven learning effects, explaining why the ordering reflects both cognitive depth and motivational cost.
> 
> **Answer:** Generation (pause, produce) → Testing Effect (retrieve, tolerate failure) → Spacing (tolerate forgetting intervals) → Interleaving (tolerate productive confusion) → Elaboration (sustain effortful connection-making) → Self-Explanation (confront comprehension gaps) → Metacognitive Strategy (regulate all others honestly). Each requires progressively more: more working memory demand, more tolerance of aversive experience, more SRL phase engagement, and more motivational infrastructure. The hierarchical ordering means lower strategies are prerequisite motivational entry points for higher strategies.
> 
> **Source:** Section 3: The Seven Learning Strategies
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #learning-effects #hierarchy #desirable-difficulties

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the desirable difficulties paradox, and why does it create a persistent motivational problem?
> 
> **Answer:** The desirable difficulties paradox is the finding that the conditions which feel most like productive learning (massed practice, re-reading, blocked practice) are among the least effective for long-term retention, while conditions that feel like ineffective or failed learning (spaced practice, retrieval failure, interleaving confusion) are the most effective. The persistent motivational problem is this: learners use subjective sense of learning as their primary feedback signal for strategy effectiveness, but this signal is systematically inverted for the strategies that matter most.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.4: Cognitive Load and the Learning Process
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #metacognitive-distortion #strategy-selection

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Application):** You have just completed reading a complex 5000-word report on cognitive science. Design the optimal post-reading protocol using specific learning effects to maximize long-term retention.
> 
> **Answer:** (1) **Free recall test** (testing effect at maximum): Close the report and write everything you can recall, without aids, for 15 minutes. (2) **Gap review** (generation + self-explanation): Open the report only to check against your recall; for each gap, generate a self-explanation of why you missed it and create a permanent note. (3) **Connection mapping** (elaboration): Open your vault and create bidirectional links from the new material to at least 3-5 existing permanent notes, writing 1-2 sentences explaining each connection. (4) **SRS card creation** (spacing + retrieval practice for future): Create Anki cards from the key concepts, ensuring question forms require generation rather than recognition. (5) **Set spaced review intervals**: Schedule a retrieval test at 1 day, 3 days, 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month from reading.
> 
> **Source:** Section 7.2: Strategy Deployment in the Reading-to-PKB Workflow
> **Difficulty:** Application
> **Tags:** #reading-practice #pkb-workflow #seven-strategies

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Connection):** What is the relationship between the basic psychological needs (SDT) and mastery goal adoption (Achievement Goal Theory)?
> 
> **Answer:** Need satisfaction and mastery goals are bidirectionally reinforcing. Autonomy need satisfaction predicts mastery goal adoption: learners who experience their study as self-chosen and personally meaningful define success in terms of developing genuine understanding rather than social performance. Competence need satisfaction supports mastery goals by calibrating challenge to the growing edge, keeping failure informative rather than devastating. Conversely, mastery goals support need satisfaction: orienting toward competence development creates the contexts in which genuine improvement (competence need) and self-chosen challenge (autonomy need) are most likely to be experienced. Performance goals tend to frustrate both needs by making success contingent on social comparison rather than genuine development.
> 
> **Source:** Section 4.2: Achievement Goal Theory
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #self-determination-theory #achievement-goal-theory #motivation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Definition):** What is the Fluency Illusion, and what is its primary consequence for self-regulated reading?
> 
> **Answer:** The fluency illusion is the systematic tendency to mistake the cognitive ease of re-reading (increased familiarity = easier processing = faster reading) for a reliable signal of learning depth. The primary consequence for self-regulated reading is monitoring corruption: the monitoring phase of the SRL cycle receives false positive signals (high comprehension estimates) from re-reading, preventing the control phase from triggering the strategy adjustments (retrieval practice, self-explanation) that would actually produce durable encoding. Learners who rely heavily on re-reading are operating with calibrated monitoring for the wrong signal.
> 
> **Source:** Section 2.5: Prior Knowledge and the Fluency Illusion
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #fluency-illusion #metacognitive-monitoring #self-regulated-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[motivation-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question (Distinction):** Distinguish between the motivational costs of the testing effect, the spacing effect, and the interleaving effect. Why is each difficult for a different reason?
> 
> **Answer:** **Testing effect**: Motivationally costly because it provides immediate, undeniable evidence of forgetting and incompetence — it directly confronts the self-efficacy of learners with performance goals. **Spacing effect**: Motivationally costly because it requires deliberately *tolerating* forgetting between sessions — the subjective experience of apparently losing progress between reviews feels like evidence the strategy is failing, when it is actually the mechanism of its success. **Interleaving effect**: Motivationally costly because interleaved practice consistently produces *lower* subjective confidence and *lower* apparent performance than blocked practice during the session itself, even though it produces better long-term transfer. The interleaving problem is the starkest inversion of the performance-mastery feel: you feel less competent during the strategy that actually makes you most competent.
> 
> **Source:** Sections 3.2, 3.3, 3.4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties #testing-effect #spacing-effect #interleaving

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is a schema?
> **A:** An organized mental framework that represents knowledge about a concept, domain, situation, event, role, or procedure. Schemas have *structure* (slots with constraints on filler values), *generativity* (they generate predictions and inferences beyond explicit input), *selectivity* (they direct attention and filter information), and *hierarchy* (they are embedded in more abstract schemas). The fundamental unit of organized knowledge in Schema Theory.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is the difference between accretion, tuning, and restructuring?
> **A:** Three qualitatively different modes of schema modification: **Accretion** = adding new facts to existing schema slots without changing schema structure (cognitively cheap). **Tuning** = adjusting schema parameters — constraints on slot values, strengths of defaults — based on accumulated feedback (the primary mechanism of deliberate practice). **Restructuring** = creating new schemas or radically reorganizing existing schemas at the structural level (cognitively expensive, often resisted, the mechanism of conceptual change).

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: Why is human memory reconstructive rather than reproductive?
> **A:** Because what is stored is a *partial trace* organized by whatever schemas were active at encoding, not a complete recording. At retrieval, this partial trace scaffolds reconstruction using: the reactivated encoding schema, default value assignment to fill gaps, contextual plausibility inferences. The result is a plausible reconstruction influenced by knowledge, expectations, and post-encoding experience — not a playback of a stored record. This is why memories are systematically distorted toward schema-consistent default values over time.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is the worked example effect, and what is its schema-theoretic explanation?
> **A:** The finding that novice learners consistently learn more from studying solved problems (worked examples) than from solving equivalent problems on their own. Schema-theoretic explanation: schema construction is the learning goal; worked examples provide explicit organized structure from which schemas can be induced. Unaided problem-solving for novices who lack relevant schemas produces cognitive overload (no schema to guide search) with minimal schema construction opportunity.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What does cognitive conflict trigger in Piaget's account, and how does it map onto schema theory?
> **A:** Cognitive conflict = the state of disequilibrium produced when new experience cannot be assimilated into existing schemas. It triggers *accommodation* (Piaget) = *restructuring* (Rumelhart/Norman) — modification of the schema's structural organization to fit the new, schema-resistant experience. Schema theory's implication for education: learners with incorrect schemas (misconceptions) will not restructure from mere informational correction; instruction must create vivid, inescapable demonstrations of the existing schema's failure before scaffolding construction of the correct framework.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is default value assignment, and why does it cause memory errors?
> **A:** Default value assignment is the automatic filling of empty schema slots with typical expected values during comprehension. It causes memory errors because: the filled default values are represented in the situational model produced during comprehension; over time, these default inferences become indistinguishable from explicitly stated information; at recall, default-value inferences are frequently misremembered as having been explicitly present in the original material (false recognition), and schema-consistent items NOT present are frequently falsely remembered as having been seen (false alarms).

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: How do Schema Theory and Cognitive Load Theory interact to explain the expertise advantage?
> **A:** CLT: expert performance is more efficient because it requires less intrinsic cognitive load. Schema Theory: expertise is constituted by well-organized, highly automated domain schemas. Integration: well-established schemas enable *chunking* — compressing many individual elements into a single schema-organized unit in working memory — dramatically increasing functional WM capacity. Schema automation reduces the cognitive load of deploying the schemas themselves. Together: expertise is high-schema richness + high-schema automation = low cognitive load for domain-typical processing.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is the encoding specificity principle and how does Schema Theory explain it?
> **A:** Encoding specificity (Tulving & Thompson, 1973): memory retrieval is most effective when retrieval conditions reinstate the contextual conditions active at encoding. Schema-theoretic explanation: retrieval involves reinstating the schemas that organized encoded material; the organized schemas act as a retrieval scaffold for the partial memory trace. When retrieval context reinstates the encoding schemas (same physical environment, same emotional state, same conceptual context), the relevant organizational frameworks are pre-activated and provide retrieval support. Context-dependence and state-dependence of memory follow directly.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: Why is prior knowledge the strongest predictor of new domain learning?
> **A:** Because prior domain knowledge constitutes domain *schemas*, and schemas are the cognitive prerequisite for new learning — not merely a helpful resource. Schemas: (1) provide organizational frameworks within which new information receives immediate structural homes; (2) enable efficient chunking that extends effective WM capacity; (3) generate productive inferences that fill in unstated implications; (4) direct attention toward schema-relevant (i.e., important) information; (5) create rich associative networks that provide multiple memory retrieval routes. Without relevant schemas, new information cannot be organized, meaningfully connected, or reliably retained.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is the generativity problem for schema theory?
> **A:** The tension between schemas being simultaneously abstract enough to generalize to novel instances and specific enough to generate substantive predictions about any particular instance. Schemas that are too specific don't transfer; schemas that are too general don't constrain. The question of how people acquire appropriately calibrated schemas (right level of abstraction) from specific experience, and how the cognitive system determines which schema applies in which context, remains a key theoretical open question in Schema Theory.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is the schema-activation account of reading comprehension failure?
> **A:** Reading comprehension requires that relevant schemas be activated at the time of processing (not after); without relevant schema activation, text elements cannot be organized into a coherent situation model. This explains: (1) Bransford & Johnson's finding — relevant topic schema must be given before reading, not after, to support comprehension; (2) why comprehension is domain-specific — fluent general readers fail to comprehend texts in unfamiliar domains because they lack domain schemas; (3) why background knowledge interventions (schema pre-activation) dramatically improve comprehension for learners with weak domain schemas.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: How does Schema Theory explain the expert-novice difference in problem categorization?
> **A:** Experts organize domain problems by *deep structural features* (underlying principles that determine solution procedure); novices organize by *surface features* (perceptual or contextual characteristics visible without domain analysis). This difference is a schema organization difference: expert schemas are indexed by the structural principles that actually govern domain problems; novice schemas are indexed by surface-level features that are irrelevant to problem solution. Chi, Feltovich, and Glaser (1981) physics study: experts categorized "conservation of energy" problems together regardless of surface differences; novices grouped "inclined plane" problems together regardless of structural principles.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Q:** Q: What is the situated or dynamic schema view, and how does it challenge classical schema theory?
> **A:** The view (Barsalou; situated/embodied cognition programs) that schemas are not fixed, stored structures retrieved in their entirety but are dynamically constructed in each situation from stable underlying representational components. This challenges the classical view that schemas are stable long-term memory structures that are either activated or not. The practical implication: schema-based effects (default value assignment, schema-guided attention, etc.) are probabilistic tendencies whose strength is modulated by situational factors — goals, emotional state, contextual constraints — not fixed laws that operate identically across contexts.

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the testing effect, and why does it produce better retention than re-studying?
> **A:** The testing effect is the finding that retrieving information from long-term memory produces stronger, more durable traces than equivalent re-studying. It works because: (1) retrieval builds the retrieval pathway specifically; (2) elaborative retrieval enriches encoding; (3) failed retrieval creates error-correction opportunities; (4) it is a desirable difficulty that engages consolidation processes.
> **Source:** Section 4, [[Testing-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fluency illusion, and why is it a metacognitive problem?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is using processing ease (how smoothly text is read) as a cue for comprehension depth (how deeply it has been understood). It is a metacognitive monitoring error: the wrong signal (fluency) is feeding the judgment-of-learning system, producing systematic overconfidence in understanding and premature termination of study.
> **Source:** Sections 1 and 7, [[Fluency-Illusion]]
> **Difficulty:** Basic

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is a desirable difficulty, and what distinguishes it from undesirable difficulty?
> **A:** A desirable difficulty impedes performance during acquisition but produces better long-term retention and transfer by engaging constructive and reconstructive memory operations (retrieval, elaboration, discrimination). Undesirable difficulty impedes performance *without* engaging these operations — e.g., cramped fonts, poor organization, cognitive overload beyond competence zone.
> **Source:** Section 2, [[Desirable-Difficulties]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** How do spacing and retrieval practice interact, and why is the interaction important?
> **A:** Retrieval practice is substantially more powerful when combined with spacing: testing at a delay requires actual retrieval from long-term memory (implicit retrieval practice embedded in spaced study), while testing immediately draws on working memory. The combination — retrieval at optimally spaced intervals — is the theoretical foundation of spaced repetition systems.
> **Source:** Sections 4 and 5, [[Spaced-Repetition]], [[Retrieval-Practice]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the interleaving effect, and why does it feel less productive than blocked study?
> **A:** The interleaving effect is that mixing problem types or topics within a study session produces better long-term retention and transfer than studying each type exhaustively before moving to the next. It feels less productive because it prevents the shallow repetition fluency that blocked study generates — requiring re-encoding from scratch on each encounter, which is the source of both the felt difficulty and the learning benefit.
> **Source:** Section 5, [[Interleaving-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** According to Self-Determination Theory, what are the three basic psychological needs and their relevance to academic reading engagement?
> **A:** Autonomy (experiencing reading as self-chosen, identity-aligned), Competence (experiencing genuine mastery growth through challenge calibrated to current ability), and Relatedness (experiencing connection to intellectual community and purpose). All three must be satisfied for reading engagement to be sustainable and autonomously motivated rather than compliance-driven and fragile.
> **Source:** Section 8, [[Basic-Psychological-Needs]], [[Self-Determination-Theory]]
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and how does it differ from retrieval practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the finding that self-generated information is encoded more durably than received information. Unlike retrieval practice (which retrieves already-encoded information), generation occurs *during initial encoding* — before durable storage. Both engage active construction, but at different points in the learning cycle: generation at encoding, retrieval practice during maintenance.
> **Source:** Section 3, [[Generation-Effect]]
> **Difficulty:** Advanced

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[strategic-reading-systems-foundational-report-2026-04-10]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
